삼성바이오로직스 (207940/KS) 
3Q26 Pre: 파업과 환율 부담에도 컨센 부합 
2026-10-06

제약/바이오. 이선경 / seonkyoung.lee@sks.co.kr / 3773-9089

매수(유지) 
목표주가: 2,000,000 원(유지) 
현재주가: 1,354,000 원 
상승여력: 47.7%

Signal: 파업과 환율 부담에도 컨센 부합 전망  
Key: 5 공장 매출 본격화, 미국 공장 매출 기여에 따른 성장 기대   
Step: 단기 불확실성보다는 중장기적 성장 방향성에 주목

3Q26 Pre: 파업과 환율 부담에도 성장지속, 컨센 부합 전망   
26년 3분기 연결기준 매출액은 1조 3,741억원(+9.3%YoY, +4.0%QoQ), 영업이
익은 6,098억원(-3.7%YoY, +4.0%QoQ, OPM 44.4%)으로 컨센서스 매출액 1
조 3,450억원과 영업이익 6,063억원에 부합하는 실적을 전망한다.  
5월 파업 영향으로 약 20개 배치 생산분(약 1,500억원)에 대한 원가 손실이 인식되
고 3분기 평균 환율이 전분기 대비 약 5% 하락했음에도 불구하고, 3분기부터 매출로 
인식되는 5공장 PPQ 배치 및 미국 공장의 매출 기여로 지속적인 성장이 예상된다. 약 
1,500억원 규모의 생산 차질분은 고객사와 일정 조율을 통해 출하될 예정으로 이에 
따른 4분기 매출 성장을 기대해 볼 수 있다.   
신규 수주 회복과 모달리티 확장에 주목 
시장에서 우려했던 신규 수주는 관세 이슈로 고객사의 컨택이 감소하면서 올해 저조했
으나 4 월 Section 232 결과 발표 이후 논의가 회복 단계에 있어 연내 추가 신규 수주 
가능성은 열려 있다.  
항체 시장의 경쟁심화와 차세대 모달리티 확장에 대응하기 위해 결정한 폴리펩타이드
의 인수는 연내 마무리될 예정이다. 폴리펩타이드의 2028 년 목표 EBITDA 마진은 
25%로 동사 대비 낮은 상황이나, 펩타이드 기반 CDMO 시장 성장에 따른 마진 개선
도 가능하다. 현재 임단협, 환율, 미국 공장 및 폴리펩타이드 인수에 따른 회계 영향 등 
불확실성이 큰 상황이나, 수주 회복과 5 공장 매출 인식 본격화, 미국 공장 및 멀티 모
달리티 확장 등 중장기 성장 방향성에 주목할 필요가 있다.

Document 141: [329180] HD현대중공업(329180) 발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다HD현대중공업(329180) 발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[329180] HD현대중공업(329180) 발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다HD현대중공업(329180) 발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

00

HD 현대중공업 (329180/KS) 
발전 엔진으로 또 성장한다 
기다렸던 4 행정 중속 엔진 캐파 증설 단행 
HD현대중공업의 4행정 엔진 캐파는 기존 3.0GW에서 증설이 완료되는 '28년부터 
램프업을 거쳐 '30년 7.2GW까지 확대될 계획이다. ①[육상 발전용]은 신공장(울산 
온산) 3GW 와 HD 현대엔진(전남 영암) 1GW 를 합쳐 4.0GW, ②[선박용]은 
2.3GW에서 3.2GW로 확대된다. HD현대엔진 캐파는 기존 0.7GW에서 1GW까
지 확대되며, 해당 라인은 신공장이 가동되기 전 ‘27~’28 년 구간에서의 선박&육상 엔
진 모두 대응 가능하다. 신공장 캐파는 총 3.0GW로, ‘28년 약 30%의 가동률로 시
작해 램프업을 거쳐 ‘30 년부터 풀캐파로 20MW급 모델을 생산할 예정이다. 4 행정 
중속엔진에 대한 수요 가시성은 높다. 동사가 공유했던 육상 발전 엔진 인콰이어리 규
모는 기존 약 3~9GW였으나, 현재는 이를 크게 상회하는 수준으로 소통하고 있다.

최근 부유식 데이터센터(FDC)에 대한 하이퍼스케일러들의 인콰이어리 또한 확대되고 
있다. HD 한국조선해양은 최근 미국 선급(ABS)로부터 100MW급 규모의 부유식 발
전 설비(BMPP)에 대한 기본 인증(AiP)를 획득했으며, 동사는 FDC 신조 사업과 더불
어 발전원 4 행정 엔진을 파워십 및 부유식 발전설비(BMPP)에 납품하며 그룹사들과 
패키지 사업으로 진행할 예정이다. 가시화된 수요를 기반으로 엔진기계사업부의 구조적 
성장 보여줄 것으로 전망하며, 이에 따른 리레이팅을 예상한다.

투자의견 ‘매수’ 및 목표주가 기존 97 만원 유지 
HD 현대중공업에 대한 투자의견 ‘매수’ 및 목표주가 기존 97 만원을 유지한다. 동사는 
올해 상선수주 목표 이미 초과달성하며 안정적인 펀더멘털 유지 중이다. 하반기 남은 
기간은 LNGC, VLGC/VLAC 중심의 선별 수주 전략을 통해 ‘28 년 이후의 상선 부
문 수익성 유지 충분히 가능할 것으로 예상된다. 글로벌 지정학적 리스크 확대에 따른 
해군 현대화 사이클 기조에 맞추어 미 해군은 동맹국의 호위함 구매를 고려하고 있으
며, MASGA를 기반으로 중장기적으로 미국 전투함을 건조하기 위해 HD현대의 미국 
진출 가능성 높다는 판단이다. 또한 미국을 제외한 글로벌 함정(페루, 필리핀, 말레이시
아, 칠레, 사우디, 이집트 등) 수주 파이프라인도 다수 보유하고 있어 함정 모멘텀에 따
른 리레이팅 충분히 가능할 전망이다. 특수선+4 행정 엔진+FDC+SMR 등 조선소에 
붙일 수 있는 모든 추가 성장동력을 보유한 유일한 종목이기에 조선업 최선호주(Top-
Pick)로 제시한다. 최근 불거지고 있는 노조 파업 문제 해소될 시 저점 매수 기회라는 
판단이다.

작성자(한승한)는 본 조사분석자료에 게재된 내용들이 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하고 있으며, 외부의 부당한 압력이나 간섭없이 신의성실하게 작성되었음을 확인합니다. 
본 보고서에 언급된 종목의 경우 당사 조사분석담당자는 본인의 담당종목을 보유하고 있지 않습니다. 
본 보고서는 기관투자가 또는 제 3 자에게 사전 제공된 사실이 없습니다. 
당사는 자료공표일 현재 해당기업과 관련하여 특별한 이해 관계가 없습니다. 
종목별 투자의견은 다음과 같습니다.

Document 146: [373220] LG에너지솔루션(373220) 보상금 제외해도 호실적LG에너지솔루션(373220) 보상금 제외해도 호실적보상금 제외해도 호실적 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[373220] LG에너지솔루션(373220) 보상금 제외해도 호실적LG에너지솔루션(373220) 보상금 제외해도 호실적보상금 제외해도 호실적 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-10-12
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

(373220) 
LG 에너지솔루션 
2026.10.12

에너지/인프라/배터리 황성현, Ph.D. 
6878 / tjdgus2009@eugenefn.com

보상금 제외해도 호실적 
 투자의견 BUY(유지) 
 목표주가 530,000원(상향) 
 현재주가 401,000원(10/8)

 [투자의견] 당사는 LG에너지솔루션에 대해 실적 추정치 상향을 반영하여 목
표주가를 530,000원으로 13% 상향하고, 투자의견 BUY를 유지한다. 
3Q26부터 ESS 물량 증가에 따른 가동률 상승 효과가 본격적으로 실적에 
반영되기 시작했으며, 4Q26에도 AMPC 제외 흑자 기조를 이어갈 전망이
다. 전사 가동률은 3분기 65%까지 상승한 것으로 파악되며, 이에 따른 펀
더멘털 개선이 본격화되고 있다고 판단한다. 
 [3Q26 잠정 실적 Review] 매출액 9.6조원(+28%qoq, +59%yoy), 영업이
익 7,560억원(+567%qoq, +26%yoy)을 기록하며 당사 추정을 큰 폭으로 
상회했다. 북미 생산보조금(AMPC)을 제외한 매출액은 9.2조원, 영업이익은 
3,391억원(영업이익률 3.7%)으로, 2 분기(-1,277억원, -1.7%) 대비 흑자 
전환에 성공했다. ① 원통형은 테슬라향 물량 호조에 힘입어 영업이익 
2,010억원(영업이익률 8.1%)을 달성한 것으로 추정된다. ② EV는 유럽 폴
란드 공장의 가동률 상승과 GM 얼티엄의 8월 가동 개시로 매출이 증가했
다. 여기에 완성차 업체 보상금 3,000억원이 반영되며 영업이익 1,090억
원(영업이익률 2.5%)을 기록한 것으로 추정된다. ③ ESS는 매출액이 50% 
이상 급증하며 실적 서프라이즈를 견인했다. AMPC 포함 영업이익은 4,460
억원(영업이익률 16.0%)으로 추정된다. 2 분기 매출 성장률 +17%qoq에 
이어 3분기 성장세가 더욱 가팔라졌다는 점이 긍정적이다. 
 [4Q26 Preview] 매출액 8.0조원(-17%qoq, +30%yoy), 영업이익 6,790
억원(-10%qoq, 흑전yoy)으로 추정한다. 4 분기 실적은 ESS 수익성 개선 
속도와 완성차 업체 보상금 반영 규모에 따라 변동할 전망이다. 동사는 기존 
가이던스를 통해 하반기 북미 ESS 생산량이 상반기 대비 2배 증가할 것으
로 제시한 바 있다. 3분기 ESS는 AMPC를 제외하더라도 양호한 수익성을 
기록한 것으로 추정된다. 향후 팩 생산 병목 해소와 북미 공장 가동 안정화
에 따른 고정비 부담 완화가 이어지며 추가적인 손익 개선이 가능할 전망이
다. EV 역시 GM 얼티엄 생산 확대와 유럽 공장 가동률 상승이 지속되며 
AMPC 제외 적자 규모를 점진적으로 축소할 것으로 예상한다. 
 [투자포인트] 9월 미시간 랜싱 공장의 개소식이 개최됐다. 랜싱 공장은 당초 
EV용 파우치 배터리 생산거점으로 계획됐으나 ESS 중심으로 역할이 재편
됐으며, 내년에는 10GWh 이상의 각형 LFP 생산라인이 추가될 예정이다. 
2027년에는 신규 대규모 증설보다 기존 북미 생산거점의 가동률 상승과 
ESS 생산량 확대를 통한 수익성 개선이 핵심이 될 전망이다. 북미 전력망 
접속 지연과 AI 데이터센터의 부하 변동성 확대에 따라 Stand-alone ESS, 
BTM, UPS, BBU 수요가 증가하면서 신규 수주도 지속 확대될 것으로 예상
한다. 특히 동사의 Non-PFE 요건 충족과 SI· O&M을 아우르는 End-to-
End 솔루션 역량이 북미 ESS 시장에서 경쟁력으로 작용할 전망이다.

당사는 동 자료를 기관투자가 또는 제 3 자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다 
조사분석담당자는 자료작성일 현재 동 종목과 관련하여 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다 
동 자료에 게재된 내용들은 조사분석담당자 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하고 있으며, 외부의 부당한 압력이나 간섭 없이 작성되었음을 확인합니다 
동 자료는 당사의 제작물로서 모든 저작권은 당사에게 있습니다 
동 자료는 당사의 동의 없이 어떠한 경우에도 어떠한 형태로든 복제, 배포, 전송, 변형, 대여할 수 없습니다 
동 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료 및 정보로부터 얻어진 것이나, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 따라서 어떠
한 경우에 동 자료는 고객의 주식투자의 결과에 대한 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙자료로 사용될 수 없습니다

Document 172: Allianz_2026-10-09_Toasted, not burnt France s bond sell-off and the road to the 2027 election_99a3202d

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-10-09_Toasted, not burnt France s bond sell-off and the road to the 2027 election_99a3202d.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-10-09
Pages: 9
Status: ok

### Page 1

In Summary 
• France is the hardest hit in the global bond sell-off, mostly due to its own fiscal and political 
weakness. Since the Hormuz crisis began, 10-year OAT yields have risen 150bps (US +115bps, 
Italy +132bps, Germany +83bps) as the prolonged energy crisis exacerbates fiscal concerns in 
a politically fragile environment. Franc e’ fiscal deficits have remained above 5% of GDP for 
four consecutive years. Debt has risen from 98% of GDP in 2019 to over 119% in Q2 2026, and 
the 2026 deficit is now expected at -5.4% against a -5% initial target. 
• A "short Europe" trade amplifies the move and spreads it to the rest of the Eurozone while 
the ECB looks the other way. Foreign banks and non-banks hold 36% of OATs and Japanese 
investors about 5% (EUR137bn), exposing France to the repatriation trade and price-sensitive 
investors. Speculative shorts, hedge -fund asset -swap unwinds and thin liquidity feed a 
negative loop, w ith market depth more than 50% below normal and the OAT spread over 
Bunds peaking at 150bp s. The speed reflects a positioning shock, not a pure repricing of 
fundamentals. Long -term investors are sitting out ahead of the elections, given budget 
uncertainty and the sensitivity of OATs to political fragility. Spanish and Italian spreads are 
about 20bps wider, bank CDS are widening and French and Italian three -month bills are 
converging toward the ECB's marginal lending rate. Fragmentation is far from 2012 or 2020 
levels, but the ingredients are in place. The ECB has not yet intervened and may limit  itself to 
verbal intervention, while continuing QT and pushing through two more hikes (+50bps).  
• Baseline: A watered -down budget, but the fiscal arithmetic stays heavy for France .  
We expect GDP growth of +0.5% in 2026 and +0.9% in 2027. The Assembly votes on the budget 
on 20 and 27 October, and we expect a budget by year -end through ordinances or Article 
49.3, keeping the 2027 deficit at -5.3% of GDP. This should contain fiscal slippage and stabilize 
financial conditions, with OAT spreads in the 120 -150bps range. Higher rates  (+100bps) will 
feed through over the next three to six months, adding EUR25bn (0.8% of GDP) in gross 
interest expenses for households, non -financial firms and the government combined. The 
debt-stabilizing deficit is -2.7% of GDP, which requires savings of more than  EUR80bn, plus 
EUR15-20bn a year to stop social and interest spending from widening the deficit. This takes 
credible fiscal measures (better tax mix, higher retirement age) and growth -enhancing 
reforms. 
• Adverse scenarios: France-centered crisis or Eurozone crisis? The Middle East situation is far 
from contained; oil prices could stay above USD100/bbl for another six months , leading to a 
further global bond sell -off hitting France the hardest, tightening financial conditions and 
jeopardizing deficit reduction. Domestically, opposition parties could take down the 
government or slippage surprises resurface despite a passed budget, tightening conditions in 
the same way. In this context, GDP would stall at +0.0% in 2027 and spread s reach 150 -
175bps. The ECB would talk up and potentially launch repo intervention. In our view, it would 
take a substantial Eurozone -wide contraction (France -0.5% in 2027 ; Eurozone -0.2%; OAT 
spreads at 175 -200bps) for the ECB to tweak or stop QT, launch open market and repo 
intervention and stabilize funding markets (as OATs and BTPs make up 50% of euro repo 
collateral). 
• Looking ahead, the 2027 elections are the next test, and TPI is a late and uncertain 
backstop. With the presidential election on 18 A pril and 2 May 2027 (legislative elections 
likely to follow), we see four scenarios: i) a President with an absolute majority and allies, who 
delivers fiscal consolidation; ii) a President short of a majority who seeks alliances  (our 
baseline, with the 2028 deficit at -4.9% and spreads above 100bps) ; iii) a President short of a 
majority without alliances  and iv) a political and fiscal crisis, for instance from unfunded tax 
cuts or spending hikes, with spreads above 200bps and widespread contagion. Only in t his 
last scenario would the ECB activate the Transmission Protection Instrument (TPI). The risk is 
a lack of political will rather than of instruments, and redenomination risk is the tail case.

Toasted, not burnt: France’s bond sell-off  
and the road to the 2027 election

### Page 2

France is the country most affected in the global bond sell-off

Since the start of the Hormuz crisis,  yields on 10 -year French government bonds (OAT) have risen by 150bps  
(vs 115bps for the US, 132bps for Italy and 83bps for Germany), the highest jump among developed economies. 
The feedback loop between rising oil prices and interest rate expectations  has met structurally vulnerable public 
finances whose credibility has been increasingly undermined by growing political fragility since the 2024 dissolution 
of the National Assembly. France’s debt-to-GDP ratio has surged from 98% in 2019 to over 119% in Q2 2026, with 
over 121% of GDP expected in 2027 (vs 83% to 87% in the Eurozone). Meanwhile, the  weakening economy (+0.5% 
GDP growth expected this year)  is leading to a fourth consecutive  year of fiscal slippage : The government now 
expects a deficit of -5.4% of GDP against the -5.1% targeted in the 2026 Budget. With Moody's upcoming review on 
23 October, France is now at risk of losing its AA rating (currently Aa3 by Moody’s vs A+ by S&P and Fitch Ratings, 
i.e. already one notch below); current pricing, if sustained, would even imply a BBB rating.

Table 1: Macroeconomic and fiscal forecasts for France in 2027

Government Allianz Research 
Real GDP growth (%) 1.0 0.9 
Inflation (CPI. %) 1.8 2.2 
Public balance (% GDP) -5.0 -5.3 
Primary balance (% GDP) -2.1 -2.5 
Debt service costs (% GDP) 2.9 2.8 
Public debt (% GDP) 121.7 121.8

Sources: French government, Allianz Research

France is the first victim of the Japanese repatriation trade.  More than other European sovereigns, France's 
investor base is exposed to price -sensitive players. The most volatile group, foreign banks and non -banks, holds 
36% of French government bonds. Domestic banks and non-banks, whose holdings fluctuate less, account for 32%, 
while non -price sensitive investors (domestic and foreign central banks) make up only 32%. Other European 
countries have either a stronger anchor in the domestic market (Italy  with 66% vs 47% for France ) or with foreign 
central banks ( Germany with 26% vs 16 % for France as Bunds as the primary FX reserve vehicle). Among foreign 
holders, Japanese investors play a significant role , holding around 5% of outstanding French government bonds 
(~EUR137bn). As interest rates rise in Japan, these positions become less attractive relative to  domestic holdings. 
At best they are no longer rolled over, at worst they are actively liquidated. In the recent bond sell -off France 
became the first victim of the J apanese repatriation trade, even though it plays only a minor role compared with 
the US as an overweight in Japanese investors' benchmark allocations .

Figure 1: France’s risk premium is very sensitive to changes in political fragility

### Page 3

Fiscal concerns, political fragility and investor base have created the vulnerability ; sentiment and positioning 
determine the market reaction. A "short Europe" trade has built up for some time in markets and France is identified 
as the most vulnerable link (Figure 2). A negative feedback loop is now at work. A bearish narrative around political 
fragility and fiscal risk is amplified by very large speculative short positions, sales from Japanese institutional 
investors, hedge fund unwinding asset swap positions and a l imited presence of buyers, with market depth more 
than 50% below normal. In such conditions , even small trades can move prices sharply. OAT yields have spiked 
rapidly, with the 10-year spread vs the Bund widening to a peak 150bps, rising twice as fast as during the Eurozone 
crisis. Realized OAT spread volatility has jumped to the 90th percentile. These market dynamics point to massive 
repositioning rather than a repricing of fundamentals . But as long as a stabilizing force of buyers is missing, this 
volatility can persist.

Figure 2: A Short Europe trade has built up with pressure on EGB spreads and EUR -USD

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

As c ontagion has switched side s, the European Government Bond (EGB) market could move from country -
specific fragility to general fragmentation. The OAT sell-off has already spilled over to other EGBs. Unlike 15 years 
ago, when France fell victim to contagion from the periphery, it is now the other way round. Spanish and Italian 
bond yields rose significantly, while German yields fell. Their 10-year spreads versus the Bund widened by around 
20bps. This decoupling within the EGB space is accompanied by widening bank CDS spreads, especially in France. 
Meanwhile, Euro funding market is under elevated stress. French and Italian three-month bills are converging 
toward the ECB's Marginal Lending Facility rate, the effective ceiling for Eurozone money-market rates. A break 
above the MLF would signal a disruption in funding  markets. The ingredients for a broader fragmentation of 
monetary conditions are thus in place, even if the current magnitude  remains far from those of  2012 or 2020. 
However, monetary transmission is also a function  of time: If these stretched conditions persist for an extended 
period, an intervention of the ECB will be more likely , e specially if we see a persistent decoupling of yield 
movements between core and periphery that can feed fears of redenomination risk.

What’s next?

The 2027 budget bill is facing stiff opposition at the National Assembly, putting the planned deficit reduction in 
jeopardy. Presented to the cabinet on 1 October, the draft budget (PLF) and the social security finance bill (PLFSS) 
for 2027 target a public deficit of -5.0% of GDP, down from -5.4% in 2026. The budget is essentially about preventing 
the deficit from getting worse rather than a fiscal adjustment. The government has announced  EUR43bn of new 
fiscal-measures (1.4% of GDP) , split between EUR25bn (58%) in spending restraint (including slowing spending 
compared to expected trends such as healthcare spending, central government cuts, social spending restraint, local 
authorities cuts) and EUR18bn (42%) in new revenues.

First, opposition parties are already pushing back against most of the big revenue raisers measures, including the

### Page 4

cut to the tax allowance on pensioners’ income (EUR1.4bn), the taxation of sick leave (0.5bn) and cutbacks on 
funding to local governments (EUR2.5bn). Second, the persistence of elevated energy prices and tighter financial 
conditions could pressure GDP growth next year, denting expected government receipts. The government expects 
GDP growth at +1% in 2027, while we e xpect +0.9% but with increasing downside risks. The next key milestones to 
watch will be 20th and 27 October when the National Assembly votes on the Draft budget part I (revenues) and 
the Social Security finance bill (Figure 3). The debt stabilizing fiscal deficit stands at -2.7% of GDP, half of today’s 
level, which entails an improvement of the public deficit of EUR83bn from 2027 on top of around EUR15-20bn of 
additional savings to find each year  just to prevent the deficit from rising  (to contain rising social security and 
interest spending).

Figure 3: France’s key political & budget milestones

Note: legislative elections are not yet scheduled – the National Assembly’s term runs to 2029 unless the next president dissolves 
it. Sources: LOLF art. 45, Constitution art. 47, Assemblée nationale budget calendar of 15 Jul 2026, rating agencies, Allianz  
Research

We lay out different scenarios of what could happen between now and the elections (Figure 4). Starting in the 
short-term, we envisage three broad possibilities: i) a watered-down budget passed before year-end which plans a 
stabilization or a slight improvement of the deficit, ii) a political crisis  with opposition parties taking down the 
government and iii) a re-intensification of the energy crisis.

Figure 4: Economic, political and market scenarios October 2026-April 2027

Source: Allianz Research 
In our baseline scenario, we  expect the French government will manage to pass a watered-down budget while 
the energy crisis eases,  allowing the deficit to remain in check in 2027 (-5.3% of GDP) Opposition parties do not

### Page 5

have an incentive  to soften their stance with the Presidential elections approaching on 18 April and 2 May 2027. 
But they also do not have an incentive to create a political crisis by taking down the government. Notably, the 
National Rally (Rassemblement National, RN) has expressed a preference for implementing a budget rather than 
relying on the ‘’Special Law’’ mechanism that was used in early 2025 and early 2026 In this environment, we expect 
the government to manage to pass a mini budget, by year -end, of limited fiscal effort just to keep the deficit in 
check (at -5.3% of GDP), either through executive ordinances – a budgetary mechanism never previously utilized 
under the Fifth Republic – or by triggering Article 49.3.  
In this baseline scenario, OAT spreads should enter a phase of stabilization but contagion risk remains. After the 
clean-up of derivative positions, we see 10 -year OAT spreads trading in a pre -election range of 120 -150bps. But 
the evolution of the French spread will not be isolated , with the potential to trigger a renewed contagion move.  
The French budget and election situation feeds the “short Europe trade”, and this negative sentiment dominates 
country-specific fundamentals. We also do not believe that stabilizing the French situation alone will be enough to 
bring the EGB market back into a convergence trade. Now that dislocations have emerged, EGB spreads are likely 
to become the new playground for speculative (leveraged) players looking to exploit them. With this comes 
elevated rates and FX  volatility and the risk of money market stress. We expect the ECB in this case to limit itself 
first to verbal intervention like a change in wording from "monitoring" to "ready to act" or "disorderly market 
dynamics". We see the tipping point at a 10-year OAT spread above 150bps. 
In a more adverse scenario where the energy crisis does not ease and/or France slips into another political crisis, 
a crisis emerges, either France-centered, or with spillovers and contagion to other Eurozone countries.  Under a 
scenario of renewed tensions in energy markets, the global markets selloff would intensify. Financial conditions 
would tighten more in France, weakening the economy and putting in jeopardy the deficit reduction. Alternatively, 
the government could be taken down in Parliament, triggering a grave political crisis and leading to tighter 
financial conditions. Even under our baseline scenario of a watered-down budget, fiscal slippages could re-emerge 
– for example if the economy is weaker than expected, or if the fiscal adjustment fall s short of what is planned  
(e.g. if revenue -raising measures disappoint). Additionally, under a Special Law, the risk of wider deficit could 
increase if continued through the presidential elections  in 2027, which could lead to France -centered crisis or a 
Eurozone crisis if tightening financial conditions in France spill over to other Eurozone countries . In a France -
centered crisis we would expect the French economy t o enter a mid-sized recession as tighter financial conditions 
would weigh on domestic demand (see next section) , with GDP at +0.0% in 2027. In a Eurozone crisis , we would 
expect French GDP to contract -0.5% and the whole Eurozone economy -0.2%.  
In this adverse scenario, we could see the 10-year OAT spread rising to 1 75-200bps. This would create 
widespread contagion in the EGB market and in the banking sector. In this case, it will clearly be up to the ECB not 
to let the situation escalate into a euro crisi s 2.0. Unlike in the euro crisis, when the escalation was fueled by a lack 
of instruments while the political will was there, this time the deployment of the instruments (TPI, OMT) could fail 
due to a lack of political will and institutional inertia. It wo uld be particularly precarious if markets began to price 
redenomination risk, i.e. the possibility of a country leaving the Eurozone. But we see this scenario as a tail risk. If 
the EGB and money markets move towards general fragmentation, we expect the ECB to set operational steps (QT 
tweak, temporary operations) at OAT spreads around 175bps, but only alongside broader contagion with BTP 
spreads around 150bp s and Spain and Portugal widening together. For France, a QT tweak would represent a 
relief of EUR4.8bn on average per month, equivalent to around 20% of monthly issuances since June. But the 
problem is that “Europe short traders” can also do this math and adjust accordingly. At this point the ECB would 
not only operate in the secondary bond market but a lso need to stabilize funding markets as in  French OAT and 
Italian BTP represent 50% of the EUR repo market collateral. The TPI activation would be late in the process and 
require OAT spread around 200bps with widespread and persistent contagion.

In 2027, the presidential (and likely legislative) elections  could re -escalate economic and market pressures.  
We envisage four scenarios, which we detail more in Figure 5. In a nutshell, under our baseline assumptions, the

### Page 6

newly elected President will not secure a n absolute majority when she or he provokes legislative elections.  
Nevertheless, the President will have the strongest group at the National Assembly and will benefit from strong 
popular support. In this environment, we would expect the President to negotiate with some opposition parties to 
pass a budget putting the deficit on a downward trajectory. Nevertheless, deficit reduction will be limited ( -4.9% 
GDP deficit expected in 2028) amid strong aversion for rapid fiscal tightening in Parliament and public opinion. The 
10-year spread will likely remain above 100bps. In an adverse scenario of political and fiscal crisis, for instance 
triggered by the enactment of tax cuts and/or spending hikes  not credibly funded, a France-centered or Eurozone 
crisis scenario would re-emerge. In the latter case, the ECB would likely intervene with the TPI with French spreads 
breaking 200bps and Italian spreads above 175bps.

Figure 5:  Economic, political and market scenarios after April 2027

Source: Allianz Research

In the short term, a significant easing of global yields and elevated French spreads hinges on three factors:   
1) a substantial easing of the energy crisis, which would reduce inflationary pressures, limiting or even ending 
further central bank hikes. 2) Strong and credible fiscal -consolidation measures passed by decree, though this 
remains unlikely before the presidential election. One option used previously is  reducing healthcare spending, but 
the amount of savings is limited to EUR5-10bn (Table 2). 3) A cooling US economy could moderate the global bond 
sell off, though this too seems less likely before 2027.

Table 2: Measures the French government can implement by decrees to bypass parliamentary vote

Measure Can government act without new parliamentary vote? 
Cancel already authorized State spending Yes 
Freeze/cancel some budgetary appropriations Yes 
Adjust medicine reimbursement within regulatory powers Yes 
Adjust healthcare tariffs/payment parameters Yes, within statutory framework 
Tighten implementation of existing social schemes Often yes 
Change parameters of social contribution rebates  Sometimes yes 
Abolish/reduce a statutory benefit Generally, no 
Permanently change tax rates No 
Fundamentally redesign social-contribution exemptions Generally, requires legislation 
Change the ONDAM target itself Generally, Parliament/LFSS

Sources: French government, various, Allianz Research 
In the longer-term, only a sizeable fiscal -consolidation program can ease French spreads sustainably.  
Ideally, a combination of the following would have the most benefits:

### Page 7

Credible fiscal adjustment (the least growth -hurting). On the revenue side, fiscal consolidation should rely on a 
more efficient tax mix to limit negative growth effects. An increase of  VAT rates would bring large receipts without 
hurting potential growth. Freez ing income-tax thresholds should be preferred to increase social contributions to 
limit the negative impact on labor demand and labor supply amid already high labor taxation in France. Some 
inefficient and costly tax rebates and loopholes could also be cut down. But most of the fiscal adjustment should 
be operated through expenditure cuts. An increase of the effective age of retirement – for instance through a rise 
in the number of years of contribution – would bring substantial savings and increase tax receipts. Substantial 
savings could also be enacted on public agencies and local governments’ administrative costs. In parallel, the 
government could launch a comprehensive spending review to improve efficiency  of public expenditure, 
benchmarking what has been done in the past in countries such as Canada and Sweden.  
Growth-enhancing reforms. The government could implement structural reforms which will likely support growth 
in the medium -term, reducing the need for further painful fiscal adjustment later on. A pension reform would not 
only bring higher receipts and increase savings; it would also stimulate labor supply and potential growth 1. Other 
growth-enhancing measures include a revamp of school education met hods, scaling back excessive regulation, 
incentivizing higher-skilled education and hiring through a reform of the taxation system.

The trickle-down effect of tighter financial conditions

The ongoing bond market problem could increasingly become an economic problem through tighter financial 
and credit conditions, but the economy has buffers to sustain a short -term financial shock. Higher sovereign 
interest rates have started to filter through on to consumer, mortgage and corporate funding costs, and the pa ss-
through will continue through 2027. This will tighten the credit and financial cycle, through higher borrowing costs 
on new lending, higher refinancing bills and tighter access to credit, and ultimately weaken consumer and 
investment spending. Our BIS-built Financial condition index (FCI) for France shows that overall financial conditions 
have tightened sharply recently, but from a relatively loose level (Figure 6, left). They are now close to their long -
term historical averages. Besides, the credit impu lse for households and corporations remain s supportive to date 
(Figure 6, right). During the Eurozone crisis of 2011 -13, the credit impulse turned sharply negative but GDP growth 
held up. While the latest ECB Bank Lending Survey (BLS) points to the credit impulses weakening in the next couple 
of quarters, this does not necessarily mean that GDP growth will weaken sharply. The French economy has buffers 
to withstand a negative finance shock, provided it does not last too long and does not get worse. In parti cular, 
households have high savings and corporates’ cash reserves have been rebuilt recently, though many firms struggle 
with elevated bankruptcies.

Figure 6: France’s financial conditions index (left); credit impulses & GDP growth (right)

Sources: LSEG Datastream, BIS, Allianz Research 
Note: last data point in the FCI is 5th October 2026.

rates would almost double households’ net interest income in 2026 to -EUR16.3bn from -EUR8.5bn in 2025, or the 
equivalent of 0.8% of disposable income (see Figure 5). Contrary to US or UK households, French households tend 
to lose when interest rates increase. We calculate that the interest earned on assets would reach EUR24.5bn while 
the interest paid would rise to EUR40.7bn.

Figure 7: Impact of a +100bps shock on interest rates for French households

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

For French non-financial companies, around 50.6% of debt is due to reset or mature within the next 12 months, 
well below the level in Italy, Spain, the UK and the US, where 78 to 90% is due for refinancing. However, France 
starts from the largest corporate debt stock in Europe in this sample, at around EUR2.27trn. This means that even 
with a lower refinancing share, around EUR1.15trn of debt still needs to be repriced within a year, more than in 
Germany, It aly o r Spain. In addition, the pressure is amplified by the starting level of borrowing costs. French 
corporates already face an effective interest rate of 6.1%, the highest among the European peers shown here. Under 
a +100bp s interest-rate shock, the effective pass -through would amount to roughly +89bp s, adding around 
EUR10bn to annual interest expenses. Relative to gross operating surplus, this represents an additional burden of 
about 2.1% of GOS, the highest impact across similar peers, and could shave roughl y -0.7pp off gross margins (see 
Figure 6). For the French government, a +100bps increase in the weighted average interest rates would mean +0.3% 
of GDP in 2027 (EUR 11.7bn) and +0.7% of GDP by 2035. Indeed, France has a rather long average maturity of its 
debt (>8.5 years) which protects it from very high volatility on its interest expenses.

Document 217: Cliff Notes_ searching for signal (2026-10-15)

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: searching for signal
By Sian Fenner, Ryan Wells & Illiana Jain
13:33 October 02 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
In Australia, this week’s main event was the RBA Monetary Policy Board’s (MPB) decision to raise the
cash rate by 25bps to 4.60%. This outcome was universally expected given Governor Bullock had
already communicated that upside risks to inflation had materialised – the conditionality established by

the MPB back in August for considering another rate hike.

### Page 2

Governor Bullock made explicit note of three upside risks. The Middle East conflict was emphasised as
the risk that had “materialised”, given the re-escalation and renewed surge in crude oil and refined
product prices over recent months. The RBA’s liaison warns this could lead to further pass-through of
costs and more persistent inflation pressures. On top of this, domestic capacity pressures and the data
centre/AI investment boom were also noted as upside risks that the MPB remain alert to.

In a video update mid-week, Chief Economist Luci Ellis explains why the bar for a follow-up rate hike in
November is low. We now expect a 25bp increase in November, provided there is no sudden resolution
of the Middle East conflict beforehand, or some other event that significantly lowers the outlook for
energy-related costs.
Subsequently, the August CPI revealed that underlying (trimmed mean) inflation rose 0.2% (3.4%yr),
which was slightly below the market’s expectation but spot on Westpac’s forecast. As usual, the

monthly detail was mixed, but the key drivers of underlying inflation remain centred on housing-related
components, particularly rents and new dwelling purchases. Headline inflation remained volatile, with
the unwind of the fuel excise cut causing another spike in fuel prices. We have maintained our forecast
for a 0.9% lift in trimmed mean inflation in Q3, and with upside risks building for Q4, a follow-up rate
hike in November appears most likely.

Regarding the current growth pulse, household spending surprised with a flat reading in August.
Services spend fell back following recent strength that was driven by major sporting events, but this
was offset by an increase in goods spend, particularly in fuel and electric vehicles. Together with our
latest card data for early September, the consumer spending pulse is shading a touch softer. However,
another solid outcome for business investment could once again be on the cards for Q3. August’s

goods trade data showcased another significant spike in data centre-related equipment imports, up
$4.3bn, similar in scale to Q1. While clearly import-driven, our modelling suggests that the local
economic impact of such investment is likely to be around half of the total investment.
Before moving offshore, a final note on housing. Cotality’s latest update revealed that dwelling prices
fell another 1.1% nationally in September. While Sydney and Melbourne have led the correction to

date, the fact that the other capitals are also reporting price declines clearly indicates that the
correction is deepening and broadening, as higher interest rates collide with the tax policy changes
announced in May’s budget. Sentiment effects indeed remain at play, as evinced by the continued pull-
back in investor credit growth. Westpac anticipates a peak-to-trough decline of around 7%, with tight
on-market supply limiting the extent of the correction. For more, see our latest Housing Pulse.

Offshore, there was plenty of data to dissect.
In the US, inflation eased in August despite ongoing strength in household demand and higher energy
prices. Headline PCE inflation rose 0.3%mth and 3.4%yr, while core PCE increased 0.2%mth and
3.0%yr. Encouragingly for the FOMC, the share of PCE components rising by more than 3% over the
past year, a measure frequently cited by Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh, declined to 45% from 49%,
suggesting some easing in broad-based inflation pressures. At the same time, household spending

remained robust, increasing 0.9%mth, equivalent to around 0.6%mth in real terms. That said, personal
income growth was soft, rising 0.2% in the month, signalling households opted to draw down savings.

### Page 3

The ISM manufacturing survey was stronger than the headline suggested. While the index edged down
0.1pt to 54.5, manufacturing has now expanded for nine consecutive months. There were some signs
of capacity constraints emerging, with production softened in the month but backlogs up 4.6pts to 56.4,
suggesting that firms struggled to keep pace with demand. New orders rose 1.6pts to 55.3, primarily
due to domestic demand as new export orders eased, backlogs increased. Firms responded by lifting

employment, 1.5pts to 52.7. Against this backdrop manufacturers saw a renewed acceleration in cost
pressures, with the prices paid index jumping 6.8pts to 77.9. Firms cited higher steel and aluminium
prices, elevated energy costs, rising freight charges and ongoing semiconductor shortages, with every
major industry reporting higher input costs during the month.
The past week’s data will have done nothing to bridge the clear divide across the FOMC, as evident in

this week’s busy Fedspeak. Some participants argued for further rate hikes, citing strong inflation,
diminished supply capacity and policy that is not yet restrictive. Others counselled patience, pointing to
progress on inflation and that September’s hike buys time to assess upcoming data. The one thing
both agree on is that inflation will be the primary determinant of the timing and extent of further rate
hikes. Our view remains that one further hike is likely this cycle to bring policy closer to neutral.

In China, policymakers announced another round of targeted support measures aimed at stabilising
growth and supporting the struggling property sector. The PBOC lowered rates on its Pledged
Supplementary Lending facility by 25bps to 1.5% and expanded quotas for targeted lending programs.
Authorities also introduced subsidies for eligible first-home buyers. The subsidy applies to homes with
a floor area of 120m² or less and a value below CNY1.5mn, with support capped at 1ppt of mortgage

costs, up to CNY10,000 and for a maximum term of five years. The measures complement reforms
announced in August to improve developer financing conditions and support housing completion.
However, weak credit demand and confidence remains a significant constraint, suggesting policy
transmission is still limited.
The September PMI data pointed to a modest improvement in activity, although the details were less
convincing. Manufacturing returned to expansionary territory, rising 0.9pts to 50.1, supported by

stronger production and new orders. Much of the improvement, however, reflected a rebound in prices,
with the raw materials price index rising 7.6pts and producer prices increasing 6.2pts. Labour market
conditions remained weak, with the employment index falling a further 0.6pts and remaining below 50
for almost four years. Conditions in the non-manufacturing sector also improved, with the services PMI
lifting from 49.0 to 50.2, driven largely by stronger construction activity. Input and output prices moved

higher, selling prices returned back above 50, export orders improved to 48.5 and employment
increased 0.5pts, although at 45.9 it remains firmly in contractionary territory. Overall, the surveys
suggest activity is stabilising and policy support is gaining some traction, but weak labour demand,
subdued credit growth and soft domestic demand indicate the recovery remains fragile.
In Japan, the September Tankan survey delivered another constructive signal on the economy and

reinforced the case for further policy normalisation. Business conditions improved by 2pts for large
manufacturers and 5pts more broadly, while profitability indicators strengthened and capital
expenditure intentions remained elevated. Large manufacturers expect fixed investment to increase by
11.6% in FY2026, while large non-manufacturers expect investment growth of 11.2%, pointing to

### Page 4

Related articles
continued confidence in the medium-term outlook.
Capacity indicators improved further across both manufacturing and non-manufacturing sectors, with
measures of excess production capacity becoming less negative and indicating that demand remains
sufficiently strong to keep pressure on existing capacity. Labour shortages also intensified, with
employment conditions becoming more negative across most industries and firm sizes, pointing to a

progressively tighter labour market. Inflation expectations remained anchored around 2%, providing
further evidence that Japan is moving towards a more sustainable inflation environment. Overall, the
survey was consistent with an economy operating close to capacity and supports the view that a
December rate hike remains a realistic possibility.

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing

5
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.36 / 5 October 2026ECONEWS
Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
ADVANCED ECONOMIES
EUROPEAN UNION / EUROZONE
Resilient corporate sentiment, upside inflation surprises. The Euro -
pean Commission’s Economic Sentiment Index for September showed
continued recovery in industry (+1.2 pts to -3.8, a 3.5 year high), with
a clear rebound in production and employment expectations, while

services improved further (+0.5 pt to 6.1). By contrast, household
confidence and sentiment in construction weakened further, as did
the composite index. These surveys broadly corroborate the PMIs,
with the September manufacturing PMI revised up by 0.2 pt to 52.9.
The unemployment rate held steady at a low 6.4%. Meanwhile, the
September flash inflation estimate came in above consensus at 3.8%.
The increase was driven mainly by energy (18.8% y/y, +4.5 pp) and

services (3.2%, +0.2 pp). Non-energy industrial goods inflation eased
slightly to 1.1% y/y, but the trend is firming: the 3m/3m annualised
rate is at a three-year high of 2.5%. Speaking before the data release,
ECB President Lagarde argued for a “measured response” and noted
that the increase in bond yields was delivering some of the needed
restraint. Tightening is also occurring via the cost-of-borrowing for
mortgage loans, which increased in August for the fifth consecutive

month to 3.6% (+6 bp m/m), a level unseen since September 2024.
It was unchanged for loans to non-financial corporations (3.8%).
Upcoming: ECB minutes (Thursday) and EU Trade Commissioner
Šefcovic's visit to Beijing (Thursday-Friday)
France. In the eye of the bond market storm. The government’s draft
budget for 2027, despite targeting significant adjustment (see our
detailed analysis), failed to stop the surge in the OAT yield, which
passed 4.9% on Friday morning (a high unseen since 2002), while the

OAT-bund spread reached a peak of 151 bps before easing somewhat
along with the global bond market selloff. (The peak coincided with
a statement by Bundesbank President Nagel that the ECB’s instru -
ments are focused on monetary policy transmission, not any spread
level). As expected, the draft budget law targets a deficit of 5% of GDP
(down from 5.4% in 2026), implying EUR 54 bn of savings, of which
about 2/3 coming from reduced spending. France’s independent fiscal

watchdog commended the spending moderation effort but pointed out
optimistic economic assumptions.
Meanwhile, inflation surprised on the upside: HICP inflation accele -
rated to 3.4% y/y in September (consensus: 3.1%), from 2.6% in August.
The sharp rise in energy prices (21.2%) was amplified by a 10% y/y
increase in the price of fresh food (linked to the summer drought).
Core inflation rose to 1.6% y/y in September, up from 1.3% in August
(services inflation at 2.2%; +0.3 pp m/m). The rise in industrial pro -

ducer prices accelerated to 6% y/y in August from 4.3% in July (+67%
versus +50% for refined products). Higher fuel prices also weighed on
household consumption, which fell by -0.5% m/m in August. On the
brighter side, the September manufacturing PMI was revised up by
0.3 points to 50.6.
Germany. Inflation accelerates to 3-year high. HICP inflation reached
3.3% y/y in September (vs. 2.9% in August and 3.2% expected) ac -
cording to the flash estimate (the highest since December 2023),

driven by energy prices. Core inflation rose by 2.4% (same as in Au -
gust) and services inflation edged down to 2.7% (vs. 2.8% in August).
The unemployment rate was stable in September at 6.4%.
Upcoming: August factory orders (Tuesday) and industrial production
(Wednesday)
Italy and Spain. Upside inflation surprises, business sentiment gains.
In Italy, HICP rose by 4.1% (vs. 3.2% in August), while core rose by 1.7%
(vs. 1.5% in August). In Spain, HICP inflation accelerated to 5% y/y

(vs. 4.6% in August), while core inflation reached 3.1% y/y (vs. 2.9%
earlier). Business sentiment, measured by the European Commission’s
ESI, rose slightly in both manufacturing and services in Italy, and
more significantly in Spain, where unlike in the rest of Europe, consu -
mer sentiment improved as well. Less positively, Italy’s unemploy -
ment rate unexpectedly rose to 6.2%.
UNITED KINGDOM
Bold new policy paths and upward growth revision. Prime Minister

Burnham broke several policy taboos in his Labour Party Conference
Speech, notably reconsidering Brexit and abandoning the so-called
“triple lock” guaranteeing that state pensions rise each year by the
highest of inflation, wages, or 2.5%. These proposals would be taken
up in the next Parliament. He also vowed to dismantle the legacy
of Thatcherism in public services and utilities. Real GDP growth for
Q2 2026 was revised upward to 0.5% q/q (vs. 0.4% previously, fol -

lowing 0.6% in Q1), driven by a stronger-than-expected performance
in trade and investment. Inflation expectations in the Bank of En -
gland’s Decision Maker Panel rose to 3.9% for own prices and 3.3%
for CPI one year ahead.
UNITED STATES
Solid growth, underwhelming payrolls. Q2 GDP growth was revised
up to 2.2% annualised (from 1.5%), with broad-based revisions to in -
vestment and consumption. The September ISM manufacturing in -
dex edged down (-0.1 pt to 54.5) on a drop in production. However,

new orders, a leading indicator, improved, and the employment in -
dex held at its highest level in four years. Payrolls rose by 29,000
in September, well below expectations, while the two months prior
were revised down by a combined 60,000. Job gains in healthcare
(+23k) and leisure/hospitality (+10k) were partly offset by losses in
Government (-10k). The unemployment rate rose to 4.2% (+0.1pp)
on higher labour force participation. Private hourly earnings growth

continued to slow (3% y/y, lowest since mid-2021). The August per -
sonal consumption expenditure (PCE) inflation report surprised to
the downside (3.4% vs. 3.7% expected), and core PCE was unchanged
at 3%. On the household side, the saving rate fell sharply (4.1% of
disposable income in August, from 4.6% in July), explaining the unex -
pected resilience of consumption (+0.6% in August inflation-adjusted).
All in all, this suggests the Fed will continue to raise interest rates

but doesn’t need to rush. We expect the next 25 bps hike in December.
Upcoming: September ISM services (Monday); FOMC Minutes (We -
dnesday).
JAPAN
Booming business sentiment. The Tankan survey index of large ma -
nufacturers rose to +24 (+2 pts q/q), while small and medium en -
terprises saw significant gains (+14; +5 pts q/q and +23; +5 pts q/q
respectively), driven by demand for semiconductors and production
equipment. Services sentiment weakened slightly (+35; -2 pts q/q for

large firms). Investment intentions remain strong among large cor -
porations and are showing signs of recovery in SMEs.

EMERGING ECONOMIES
Manufacturing PMIs generally stronger. The index rose to 56.7 in
Taiwan (the highest level since 2021) and 53.9 in South Korea, boosted
by strong global demand for AI goods. Korean exports increased by
a record 83.5% y/y in September (of which chips: +263% y/y), mostly
led by export prices. The manufacturing PMI also improved and was

comfortably above 50 in India, Indonesia and Thailand. It declined
slightly in Vietnam (to 51.9). Malaysia and the Philippines stood out
with PMIs falling slightly below 50. PMIs also generally improved
in central Europe. In Latin America, PMIs were more mixed: falling
sharply in Brazil to 44.8, its lowest level since early 2023, but slightly
improving in Mexico (to 50) and Colombia (to 54.6).
ASIA
China. Better outlook on small stimulus and higher PMIs. Following

a string of data showing too sluggish domestic demand to meet the
Government’s growth target of 4.5 to 5%, the State Council announced
a package of stimulus measures, including the expansion of some re -
lending facilities aimed at financing tech/innovation, agriculture and
SMEs; new spending to be financed by carried-over local govern -
ment debt quotas; and a small interest rate subsidy on mortgages.
The latest PMIs published by the NBS and by RatingDog improved

marginally, with both now showing expansion in both manufacturing
and services.
EUROPE, MIDDLE EAST
Poland. Strong uptick in September’s inflation reading. Inflation
came in at 4.0% y/y in September (the highest level since June
2025) after 3.4% y/y in August, driven by higher prices at the pump.
The increase led the government to lower its VAT rate on fuel to 8%
(23% previously) from October until year-end, which should help ease
inflationary pressures. Overall, inflation may not significantly exceed

the Central bank’s target on average in 2026. The Central Bank may
nonetheless consider one rate hike by year end.
Türkiye. A financial scandal with limited macro impact. A Ponzi sche-
me collapse has impacted around 455k retail investors with assets
reportedly worth about 1% of GDP . The crisis was swiftly handled
by the authorities to prevent financial contagion. 131 funds and two
home-finance institutions involved in the scandal were suspended

and taken over by public banks for orderly and fair disposal of their
assets. Three investment banks and 2 factoring companies were
transferred to the Savings Deposit Insurance Fund. So far, 217 people
have been subjected to legal action, of whom 90 have been jailed
pending trial. Since September 9, the BIST-30 index lost 10% but is
still 25% higher than at end-2025, and foreign investors bought equi -
ties at the highest pace in 3 months last week, suggesting a buy-the-

dip reading of the situation. There has been no or marginal spillover
effects on the exchange rate, domestic interest rates and sovereign
spreads. At this stage, contagion risk to the banking sector is very low
but the damage to small investors may have negative spillovers on
consumption through negative wealth effect. Separately, the manu -
facturing PMI fell further in September to 47.9, signaling deepening
contraction.
Saudi Arabia. Budget deficit jumps on cost of war. Budget deficit is

now forecasted at USD 65.4 bn this year (-4.9% of GDP), up from
USD 44.3 bn (-3.3% of GDP) in the initial financing bill. Revenue is
expected to be slightly higher (+3.7%) as high oil prices more than
offset the decline in oil export volume. But spending has been revised
up by 9.3%. The new growth projection is also bearish, with GDP
expected to contract by -3.6% due to the decline in oil activity while
consensus sees economy growing by +0.2%.
LATIN AMERICA

Brazil. A tight runoff, a tough policy mix inheritance. Senator Flávio
Bolsonaro and Brazil’s incumbent President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva
will face off in a runoff on October 25 th after neither won a majority
in Sunday’s general election (47% vs. 45% of the vote respectively).
Since Brazil returned to democracy in 1989, no candidate who trailed
in the first round has won the runoff. The next President will inherit
a slowing economy and a difficult policy mix: pressing ahead with

fiscal consolidation while normalising monetary policy amid recur -
ring supply shocks (adverse weather conditions, El Niño, war in the
Middle East).
ENERGY
Energy. Limited relief, electricity excepted. Re-escalation risks ap -
pear to be rising in the very short term in the Gulf and the Red Sea.
At the same time, the record high level of Gulf oil exports in Sep -
tember (above 80% of pre-war level including bypass and trade shutt-
le) has been enough to stabilize prices at a high level, albeit not to

put significant and lasting downward pressure on crude prices.
- Crude oil: prices declined slightly over the week but remain high
(-3.5% w/w to USD 101.6/b on Monday morning for Brent). The gap
with physical barrel prices (Dated Brent) is rising again USD 24.4/b on
Friday), signalling persisting tensions on the physical market.
- The diesel market got some relief from a G7 decision late on Friday
to release 100 mb of oil and diesel emergency stocks, focused on the

latter. It should take place over the next four months and reportedly
puts off the table US export restrictions. The European benchmark
fell markedly (-12% w/w to USD80/b on Monday morning). However,
market pressure could rise again soon as most Chinese refiners an -
nounced the suspension of fuel exports for October as the government
want to prioritize domestic supply security.
- EU wholesale electricity prices rose sharply in September, reaching

EUR 146/MWh on average, the highest level since Dec 2022.

October 12-16
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 11 Oct 2026

### Page 2

Photo by Monica Sauro / Unsplash

### Page 3

Last week was relatively quiet on the economic data front, but the
indicators released pointed to continued economic strength. ADP weekly
payroll gains increased for the fifth consecutive week, reaching a three-
month high, while jobless claims fell to multi-year lows. The ISM NM-PMI
remained solid at 54.9 in September, though its prices paid component
jumped to 74.0, the highest since July 2022. Redbook same-store sales
rose 8.3% y/y during the week of October 2, signaling continued strength

in consumer spending, while the New York Fed's consumer survey
showed expected spending growth and one-year-ahead inflation
expectations both reaching three-year highs.
The data suggest that the Fed has good reason to be hawkish.
Accordingly, September's FOMC meeting minutes showed that most
policymakers favored another rate hike by year-end, with several judging
monetary policy to be barely restrictive, if at all. We agree that further
tightening is warranted. However, recent comments by New York Fed

President John Williams, Fed Vice Chair Philip Jefferson, and Governor
Christopher Waller suggest flexibility on the timing. The markets assign a
17.2% probability to an October hike and a 71.6% probability of a
December hike.
This week's inflation reports could change those odds. A hotter-than-
expected CPI reading on Wednesday could prompt the markets to price in
an October hike, pressuring the Fed after September's unanimous 25bps
increase, which Fed Chair Kevin Warsh described as removing "a dose of

accommodation." An in-line or cooler CPI reading would support patience.
Warsh speaks Thursday evening at the IMF and World Bank annual
meetings in Bangkok.
Here's more:

### Page 4

(1) Inflation. The September CPI and PPI reports will be released on
Wednesday and Thursday, respectively. The Cleveland Fed’s Inflation
Nowcast model projects a hot 0.53% m/m increase in headline CPI, while
core CPI is expected to rise a more modest 0.2%. That would put headline
and core CPI inflation at 3.6% and 2.4% y/y, respectively. September is
expected to mark the 67th consecutive month of CPI inflation exceeding
2.0% y/y, underscoring the persistence of inflationary pressures (chart).

Adding to inflation concerns, the averages of the prices-paid and prices-
received indexes of five regional Fed banks’ business surveys rose to their
highest levels since 2022 in September (chart). Both have historically
tracked headline CPI inflation closely.

### Page 5

The same indicators have also closely tracked PPI final demand inflation,
which was already running hot at 5.4% y/y in August (chart).
(2) Monthly Treasury statement. The federal budget deficit totaled $1.77
trillion over the 12 months through August, while outstanding marketable
Treasury securities increased by $2.12 trillion over the 12 months through
September (chart). The recent slowdown in Treasury borrowing suggests
that the deficit may have narrowed somewhat. Tuesday's report will reveal

the final budget deficit for fiscal 2026, which ended on September 30.
(3) Consumer spending. Thursday's September retail sales report will
provide the first official reading on consumer spending for the month. We
expect another solid increase, as Redbook same-store sales rose 8.3%
y/y in September, well above the 2025 average of 5.8%. Redbook sales

### Page 6

historically have tracked official retail sales closely, suggesting that
consumer spending maintained strong momentum through the end of Q3
(chart).
(4) Manufacturing. The week will also provide important insights into the
manufacturing sector, with the October New York and Philadelphia Fed
banks’ business surveys due Thursday, followed by September's industrial
production and capacity utilization reports on Friday.
Manufacturing aggregate weekly hours rose to 84.4 in September, the

highest since August 2023, continuing their recent upward trend (chart).
Immediate expensing under the OBBBA, reshoring, and the AI buildout
are helping to reinvigorate manufacturing activity. With skilled labor in
short supply, manufacturers appear to be meeting stronger demand partly
by increasing the hours worked by existing employees. The rise in
aggregate hours worked suggests that manufacturing production
expanded at a healthy pace in September.

### Page 7

The New York and Philadelphia Fed manufacturing surveys provide
another reason for optimism. Their average manufacturing PMI rose to
22.7 in September, the highest since 2021 (chart). Historically, the
average has closely tracked manufacturing production growth, suggesting
that factory output could accelerate further in the months ahead.

Islamic finance is growing at a rapid pace, driven 
primarily by increasing demand for investors who 
seek Shariah -compliant investments. Sukuk, often 
referred to as Islamic bonds, surpassed USD 1trn in 
2025, making them one of the fastest -growing 
segments o f Islamic finance. The asset class’s 
unique value proposition is driving its popularity, 
especially in an increasingly volatile global  
economic landscape. 
In this publication, we will explore the evolving role of Sukuk in wealth management, 
examining market fundamentals, risk frameworks and emerging opportunities within 
this market.

### Page 3

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  3

Sukuk, commonly referred to as Islamic bonds, are Shariah-compliant investment 
certificates representing ownership interests in underlying assets, usufructs (the 
right to use an asset and receive its benefits, without owning it) or ventures. In 
return, Sukuk holders receive periodic distributions, typically in the form of rental 
payments (Ijarah), profit-sharing (Mudarabah) or asset-generated cash flows, and 
upon maturity the principal amount is repaid. Sukuk differ from conventional 
bonds in several ways: 
Fig. 1  
Sukuk vs. conventional bonds

Aspect  Conventional bonds Sukuk

Core principle  Lending (time-value of money) Asset ownership 
Underlying asset  Any asset, business or project Must be Sharia-compliant 
Returns  Guaranteed (fixed or floating interest) Not legally guaranteed (depends on 
underlying asset’s performance) 
Risk   Issuer bears all performance risk Shared risk between parties 
Transparency  Moderate (depends on disclosure) High (asset identification required)

Source: Standard Chartered

The role of Sukuk in the investing landscape is evolving amid 
global uncertainty and rising geopolitical tensions. Investors 
are increasingly seeking more stable and asset ‑backed 
alternatives. An investor can consider investing in Sukuk for 
the following reasons: 
Resilient 
Sukuk provide asset -backed collateral that conventional 
bonds often lack, offering a cushion during volatile market 
conditions. Historically, Islamic investments, including Sukuk’, 
have been countercyclical, i.e. they tend to expand or remain 
stable when c onventional markets contract. Across major 
market stress from the 2018 US-China Trade War through the 
2026 Middle East Conflict, Sukuk bonds consistently 
outperformed Global Bonds — delivering smaller drawdowns 
in risk-off episodes such as the 2018 US-China trade war and 
2022 higher for longer inflation, and greater resilience in 
geopolitical oil shocks, underscoring the asset class's 
structural defensive characteristics.

Strong fundamentals
The Sukuk market experienced an unprecedented 25% growth in issuance in 2025, with issuance nearly USD 300bn. According
to some  reports,  the  Sukuk  market  could  reach  USD  3trn  by  2030  driven  by  increased  demand  for  Shariah-compliant
investments, sovereign funds’ diversification needs, development of regulatory frameworks for Sukuk and growth in Middle East

capital markets.  Nearly  80%  of  Fitch ‑rated Sukuk  carry  investment ‑grade ratings  and  have  little  or  no  history  of  default,
underscoring the sector’s strong fundamentals. These factors should help to instil confidence among investors, who can look at
Sukuk as central building blocks of their portfolio.

Convergence with sustainable investing trends
Sukuk and sustainable bonds share more common ground than their different origins suggest, making sustainable Sukuk a
natural bridge between Islamic finance and sustainable investing. This also helps explain Sukuk’s growing appeal beyond
traditional Muslim investors, as evidenced by Sustainable Sukuk issuance rising from USD 464mn in 2017 to USD 9bn in 2024,

representing a CAGR of 52.7%. The overlap starts with what Sukuk exclude: Shariah principles prohibit interest, excessive
uncertainty and speculation, and screen out sectors financing harmful industries such as tobacco, gambling, weapons, adult
entertainment and alcohol – a negative screen that closely aligns with the exclusionary (negative screening) method, commonly
used in sustainable investing. Tradi tional Sukuk bonds require proceeds to be linked to identifiable real assets or projects,

creating a ring-fenced structure like use-of-proceeds sustainable bonds. Combined with Shariah’s broader objectives of social
welfare, resource stewardship and equita ble risk-sharing, this creates strong alignment with the United Nations Sustainable
Development Goals.
Green Sukuk is an established example, where proceeds are earmarked for environmental projects such as renewable energy,
clean transport, energy-efficient buildings or sustainable water and wastewater management. Issuers must meet both Shariah

approval from a recognised scholarly board and alignment with International Capital Market Association (ICMA) Green Bond
Principles.
Traditional Sukuk already comply with Shariah laws such as avoiding Riba (interest -based returns arising from lending
transactions), Gharar (investments with ambiguity) and Maysir (speculation). Sustainable Sukuk build on this foundation by
adding environmental and social objectives. The table below highlights how sustainable Sukuk differ from traditional Sukuk.

Fig. 4
Traditional Sukuk vs. Sustainable bonds vs Sustainable Sukuk
Aspect Traditional Sukuk Sustainable Bonds Sustainable Sukuk
Underlying structure Asset-linked

Digital integration
The integration  of  blockchain  technology  and  digital  platforms  is  revolutionising  Sukuk  issuance  and  trading,  dramatically
reducing transaction costs and expanding accessibility to  a broader  investor base.  For instance, SAKKEX (an  independent
blockchain-based Sukuk marketplace) streamlines drafting, issuance, and near ‑instant settlement. Its Sukuk Wallet App also
allows investors to trade on the go. Several such digital platforms now allow tokenised Sukuk that enable fractional ownership,

automated Shariah  compliance  verification  and  real-time  settlement,  thereby  addressing  challenges  that  have  persisted  in
Islamic finance markets. Digital tools also simplify cross border Sukuk transactions by enabling end to end digital origination and
trading, with smart contracts automating profit sharing to cut operational costs and enhance transparency.

### Page 6

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026 6
Sukuk structures define the nature of ownership, risk -sharing and return
generation. Several types of Sukuk structures exist and are defined by the
underlying Shariah contract. We discuss some prominent ones below and see
how they align with various phases of an investor’s wealth journey:
Sukuk al-Ijarah is a Shariah-compliant lease-based bond where the investor gains partial ownership in a physical

asset such as a building, airport or equipment. The asset is then leased back to the issuer who pays a nominal
(pre-agreed) rent to the investor. An investor seeking stable, predictable income streams with lower volatility can
consider investing in these Sukuk. The original principal amount is returned at the end of the lease period.
Ijarah Sukuk are ideal for the wealth preservation phase of an investor’s wealth journey. For instance, investors
nearing retirement typically need to restructure their portfolios by shifting from growth to preservation while

generating steady income to cover living expenses. Ijarah Sukuk deliver on both fronts: protecting capital through
asset-backed structures while providing predictable rental income that supports household needs without
depleting principal.
Sukuk al-Murabahah are cost-plus financing structures where the issuer buys an asset and immediately resells
it to the investors at a marked-up price, with payment deferred over a pre-determined time. Consider this: Let us

assume, a company needs USD 100m. A special purpose vehicle (SPV), established by the company, mobilises
funds from investors and buys commodities worth USD 100m and immediately resells them to the company for
USD 112m payable over time. The USD 12m difference is the agreed profit margin. Murabahah Sukuk ar e best
suited for investors seeking income over a short to medium term, typically, 1-5 years. Investments in this type of
Sukuk return the principal gradually throughout the investment period rather than only at maturity.

Investments in Murabahah Sukuk are best suited for wealth protection and income generation. Unlike Ijarah which
return principal as a lump sum at maturity, Murabahah amortise both profit and principal in each instalment. This
makes them ideal for retirees who need to systematically draw down capital to fund living expenses without
depleting their portfolio too quickly.
Sukuk al-Mudarabah is a profit-sharing partnership arrangement where investors provide 100% of the capital

and operate as silent partners. The issuer manages the business or project as the active partner. Under a
Mudarabah arrangement, financial losses arising from normal bu siness risks are borne by the investors in
proportion to their capital contribution, while the Mudarib loses its effort and entitlement to profit. However, where
losses result from negligence, misconduct, breach of agreed terms, or misconduct by the Mudari b, the Mudarib
may be held liable. Think of it as venture capital meets bonds; you fund the business, professionals run it and

you share the profits or bear the losses resulting from the business activities. Mudarabah Sukuk are often
regarded as one of the  structures most closely aligned with the Shariah principle of profit and loss sharing.
However, they bear higher risk and lower predictability compared to other structures.
Investors with a growth mindset and open to high -risk tolerance can consider investing in these Sukuk . They
best align with accumulation and growth phase of the wealth journey, typically, when investors have long time

horizons to absorb volatility and can afford capital risk for higher returns. However, to balance risk, it is important
for investors to diversify across various types of Sukuk. Even within Mudarabah, they can consider investing
in multiple projects, sectors and geographies.
1 Sukuk structures and
wealth mandates

### Page 7

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  7

A joint venture partnership structure where both investors and the issuer contribute capital is Sukuk al -
Musharakah. In this structure, both parties (issuer and investors) jointly provide capital, participate in managing 
the venture, and share profits or losses based on agreed upon ratios. This structure appeals to investors focused 
on growth, interested in active participation, and prepared to take on greater risk in pursuit of higher returns. 
Musharakah Sukuk are best suited for the growth and accumulation phase where investors are building wealth 
aggressively, comfortable with high risk and able to lock up capital for the long term (typically 5+ years).

Sukuk al-Wakalah is an agency arrangement where investors appoint a representative from the issuing company 
as their Wakil (agent) to invest their funds in specific Shariah-compliant assets or projects in exchange for a fee. 
This arrangement is similar to a mutual fund or ETF, where the agent manages a diversified pool of Shariah 
compliant assets on behalf of the investors. Investors benefit from professional fund management, diversification, 
transparency and flexibility. Depending on issuer preferences, Wakalah Sukuk can b e structured with lower 
investment thresholds, making them accessible to a wide range of investors. 
Wakalah Sukuk suitability depends heavily on the underlying asset allocation and risk profile. Conservative 
Wakalah structures investing in stable, income -generating assets suit wealth preservation. Moderate -risk 
Wakalah can complement accumulation portfolios as a diversification tool, though not as a primary growth driver 
as their structure and risk mandate limit upside potential.

Sukuk al-Istisna is a project financing structure based on a manufacturing or construction contract. Investors 
provide upfront capital to finance the construction of specific assets. Upon completion, the asset is sold to an end 
buyer (often the original issuer or a third party) who makes deferred payments including the cost plus a mark-up, 
which are then distributed to investors. Istisna suit seasoned investors with construction finance expertise and 
those who can absorb development risk and commit capital for the long term. Owing to its complexity and higher 
risk profile, this type of Sukuk typically requires large initial investments, making it less accessible to retail 
investors.  
In the wealth cycle, Istisna are best aligned with the accumulation and growth phase, when long time horizons 
can absorb construction delays and development risks. The long-term commitment and potential for cost overruns 
or project failure make it unsuitable for later wealth stages such as retirement.

Sukuk al -Salam is an Islamic bond backed by a forward purchase contract where investors pay upfront for 
commodities to be delivered later. At its core, they are similar to forward contracts. However, unlike a forward 
contract, Salam contracts are fully prepaid, standardi sed and designed with clearly defined delivery terms to 
reduce uncertainty (gharar). Under this arrangement, an investor profits from the difference between the lower 
Salam purchase price (paid today) and the higher resale price (received at delivery).  
Salam Sukuk carry high delivery and price risk and aligns well with investors in the growth and accumulation 
phase. Salam requires strong commodity‑market expertise, deep understanding of production and delivery risks, 
and the ability to assess the issuer’s operational capacity to deliver the specified goods on time.

Due diligence in Sukuk extends beyond traditional credit
analysis and an investor should also consider the following:
• Shariah compliance verification can be done by evaluating

the Sukuk structure and confirming that its mandates and
underlying assets fully align with Islamic principles.
• Evaluating asset quality by verifying tangibility, cash -
generating capability and legal ownership can help
investor reduce risk.
• Assessing creditworthiness and market depth will help in
identifying financially strong Sukuk issuers and ensuring
adequate liquidity.
• Cross-border Sukuk investments remain viable,
contingent on careful scrutiny of legal documentation

across multiple jurisdictions to ensure enforceability and
investor protection.
Robust due diligence and proactive risk mitigation strategies
are essential to protect capital and stay aligned with Islamic
financial principles.
1 Risk management and due diligence

### Page 10

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  10

Staying up to date on the latest developments in 
the Sukuk market is important as this space is 
constantly evolving and rapidly expanding. 
Moreover, Islamic finance is not static; Shariah 
interpretations related to Sukuk structures are 
periodically redefined. Staying informed protects 
investors from investing in non -compliant 
options. We look at some of the factors that are 
guiding the future of this landscape:

1 Emerging trends

Green Sukuk issuance is projected to grow materially through 
2030, reflecting the natural convergence of Islamic finance and 
sustainability principles. Key sectors include renewable 
energy, green buildings, low -carbon transportation, water and 
waste management and sustainable agriculture.

Tech advancements  are revolutionising Sukuk origination, 
trading and accessibility. Digitisation offers numerous benefits 
to the Islamic finance landscape: streamlined issuance and 
trading processes, enhanced accessibility through 
tokenisation, immutable audit trails via bl ockchain technology 
and improved secondary market liquidity.

A major regulatory shift is likely to create standardised rules 
across Islamic finance markets globally. The pivotal 
development is AAOIFI's Standard 62, launching in 2026, 
which mandates transparent, Shariah-compliant asset-backing 
and genuine asset ownership. This can fundament ally 
strengthen the Islamic authenticity of Sukuk structures.

The Sukuk market is rapidly expanding beyond its traditional 
Islamic finance strongholds. Non-traditional territories  in 
Africa (e.g., Algeria, Benin and Nigeria), Europe (e.g., 
Luxemburg, UK) and Asia (e.g., Turkey, Pakistan) are 
emerging as Islamic capital market gateways. This expansion 
underscores the evolution of Sukuk from a niche Islamic 
product to a globally reco gnised asset class attracting diverse 
sovereigns and investors across continents.

### Page 11

InvesTips: Sukuk: Structure, stability and strategic opportunity  |  September 2026  11

Conclusion

Ultimately, Sukuk demonstrate that Shariah compliance and sound investment 
strategy are not competing priorities, but complementary ones. By ensuring that 
portfolios are grounded in real economic activity by offering stability through 
tangible asset -backing and transparent structures that channel capital towards 
productive use. Their foundation in ethical, responsible financing appeals to 
investors seeking both resilience and purpose. As global markets increasingly 
prioritise integrity and long-term sustainability, Sukuk stand out as a compelling tool 
for Shariah -compliant investors who want meaningful diversification without 
compromising on their religious and ethical principles.
